## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Score is MINIMISED, so negative scores are better
- We need to deliver items from the GOOD half (decreases score by 1) and avoid items from the BAD half (increases score by 1)
- The champion (-11.46) uses: State 0 = Exploration (random walk), State 1 = GoAwayColor (color 2 = green, moving away from green)
- The champion's State 1 transitions: with prob 0.9692 go back to state 0, on BLACK floor (prob 0.7499) go to state 0, on perceiving color 5 (magenta) go back to state 0

Wait, let me re-read. State 1 is GoAwayColor with clr=2 (green). The transitions from state 1: 
- n1x0: D=3→ but nstates=2, so D goes to... wait with 2 states, from state 1 the only other state is state 0, so D=0 means state 0. But n1x0 D=3? That seems wrong... Actually wait, looking at the format: "With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0." But from state 1, D=0 must target state 0. The value 3 here may be valid only if it somehow maps... Actually re-reading: D is indexed among OTHER states only. With 2 states from state 1, the only other is state 0, so D=0. But the champion has D=3 and D=0... This seems like it could be a quirk of the system accepting any value. Let me not worry about it.

**Key insight from champion:** GoAway from GREEN (color 2) seems to work well. This means robots go toward BLUE side, pick up blue items, and return. The exploration state finds the source, then GoAwayColor from green pushes toward blue wall.

**Strategy:**
1. Elitism: copy champion exactly
2. Exploitation: small edits of champion (adjust probabilities, try GoAwayColor with color 1=red for nest-wall avoidance, etc.)
3. Exploration: try 3-state machines with explicit nest-return behavior, GoToColor for blue wall

**Design ideas:**
- 3-state: Explore → on BLACK floor go to GoAwayGreen → on WHITE floor (nest) go back to Explore
- 3-state: Explore → GoToBlue (color 3) → on WHITE go back to Explore
- Try GoToColor blue (clr=3) to actively seek blue side
- Try GoAwayColor red (clr=1) to move away from nest wall toward source

Let me design 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion variant: slightly adjusted probabilities
3. Champion variant: change clr from 2 to 1 (go away from red = nest wall)
4. Champion variant: add transition on WHITE floor to return to exploration
5. 3-state: Explore, GoAwayGreen, GoToColor(white/nest)
6. 3-state: Explore, GoToBlue, GoAwayRed
7. 2-state: GoToColor(blue=3), Exploration
8. 3-state with attraction + GoAwayGreen
9. 2-state: Exploration + GoToBlue
10. 3-state: Explore, GoAwayGreen, Explore(different rwm)
11. 2-state variant with different rwm
12. 3-state: GoAwayGreen with floor-based transitions
13. 4-state foraging pipeline
14. Exploration variant

## Reasoning

**Analysis of results:**
- The champion (-12.22) is a 2-state machine: Exploration (rwm=19) → GoAwayColor(green, clr=2)
- The top 5 performers all use this same 2-state topology with minor variations
- 3-state machines with extra GoToColor states performed poorly
- The key pattern: State 0 (Explore) with 3 transitions (two probabilistic + one neighbor-count), State 1 (GoAwayGreen) with 3 transitions (probabilistic + BLACK floor + perceive magenta)

**What's working:** GoAwayColor from GREEN (clr=2) means robots move away from green walls toward blue walls, picking up blue items (which decrease score). They return when on WHITE floor (nest) or probabilistically.

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Fine-tune probabilities around champion values (p0x0 ~0.9, p0x1 ~0.47, p0x2 ~8 neighbors, w0x2 ~11)
- Try slightly different rwm values (15-30 range)
- Try different transition combos in state 1 (WHITE floor instead of BLACK for return)
- Mix in floor-color conditions more carefully

**Exploration strategy:**
- Try 3-state with GoToColor(blue) as intermediate
- Try different neighbor thresholds
- Try GoAwayColor(red/clr=1) to flee nest wall toward source

Key insight: The champion's state 1 transitions back to state 0 via: probabilistic (0.9692), BLACK floor (0.7499), perceive magenta (0.8499). The BLACK floor condition makes it leave the source quickly. The magenta perception (color 5) likely detects other robots' LEDs.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of plateau:**
The champion (-12.81) hasn't improved this round. The best this round was -12.64. We're converging around the same 2-state topology. Key observations:

- p1x1=0.85 (BLACK floor return) slightly better than 0.8 in run #1 (-12.64)
- p0x2=2 (very few neighbors triggers GoAway) also good (-12.40)
- p0x2=5, w0x2=11 slightly worse than w0x2=10
- Adding 4th transition to state 1 hurt performance
- 3-state machines keep performing poorly

**Fine-tuning targets from champion:**
- p1x1: try 0.82, 0.84, 0.86, 0.88 (0.8 is champion, 0.85 was good)
- p1x2: try 0.88, 0.89, 0.91, 0.92 (0.9 is champion)
- p1x0: try 0.94, 0.96 (0.95 is champion)
- p0x2 threshold: try 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6
- w0x2: try 9, 10.5, 13

**Key insight:** The "neighbors below threshold" condition triggers when a robot is isolated/spread out. With threshold=5 and w=10, roughly half of robots will trigger this. Lower threshold (2-3) means only very isolated robots go to GoAway mode.

**Exploration:**
- Try GoAwayColor clr=1 (red=nest wall) — flee from red to go toward source
- Try different state 0 behavior (GoToColor blue clr=3)
- Try mixing p0x0 and p0x1 probabilities differently

## Reasoning

**Analysis of plateau:**
We're stuck at -12.81 for 3 rounds. The champion uses p0x0=0.85, p0x1=0.5, neighbor-below-5 w=10, then GoAwayGreen with p1x0=0.95, p1x1=0.8 (BLACK), p1x2=0.9 (magenta).

Best this round: rwm=20, p1x1=0.82, p1x2=0.91 (-12.74). The slight variation in p1x1 (0.82 vs 0.8) and p1x2 (0.91 vs 0.9) is marginally better in some runs.

**Observations:**
- The champion is at -12.81, and most variants score between -12.1 and -12.8 — we're in a very flat landscape
- p0x2=2 (threshold) was worse this round; p0x2=4 and 5 are both decent
- w0x2=9 slightly worse; w0x2=10 best
- p0x0=0.84 (slightly lower) was worse
- The scoring is noisy (stochastic), so we need multiple runs of similar configs

**Strategy:**
- Elitism: keep champion
- Exploitation: Cross-breed best performers (rwm=20 from #1, p1x1=0.82 from #1, p1x2=0.91 from #1-3)
- Try rwm=18, 21
- Try w0x2=10.5 
- Explore: try completely different topology with GoToColor(blue) as first state
- Try 2-state with GoAwayColor(red) to avoid nest

The main uncertainty is whether there's a better solution in a different topology we haven't explored enough. Let me try a few genuinely different things alongside exploitation.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of new champion (-13.05):**
The key change: p1x1=0.78 (BLACK floor return, was 0.8) and p1x2=0.89 (magenta perception return, was 0.9). Slightly lower return probabilities in state 1 means robots spend a bit more time in GoAwayGreen mode before returning. This makes sense: they go deeper into the source area.

**Observations:**
- p1x1=0.78, p1x2=0.89 is the new sweet spot (champion)
- p1x1=0.82, p1x2=0.91 scored -12.42 to -12.56 (worse)
- p1x1=0.8, p1x2=0.9 scored -11.49 (worse, noise in scores)
- p1x1=0.83 scored -11.81 (worse)
- So the optimal seems around 0.76-0.80 for p1x1 and 0.87-0.91 for p1x2
- rwm=19 still seems best (18, 20, 21 all worse this round)

**Exploitation:** Fine-tune around champion's p1x1=0.78, p1x2=0.89:
- Try p1x1=0.75, 0.76, 0.77, 0.79, 0.80
- Try p1x2=0.87, 0.88, 0.90
- Try p1x0=0.94, 0.96

**Exploration:** We've been stuck on the same topology for many rounds. Let me try a few genuinely different approaches:
- Swap state order: GoAwayGreen first, Explore second
- Try GoAwayColor(red=1) to flee nest wall
- Try adding a 4th transition to state 0 using floor color

## Reasoning

**Analysis:**
The champion (-13.05) is still p1x1=0.78, p1x2=0.89. This round's best performers cluster around:
- p1x1=0.78, p1x2=0.87 (-12.64) — slightly lower p1x2
- p1x1=0.78, p1x2=0.89, p0x2=6 (-12.52) — higher neighbor threshold
- p1x1=0.75, p1x2=0.87 (-12.40) — both slightly lower
- p1x1=0.79, p1x2=0.89 (-12.31)
- p1x1=0.77, p1x2=0.88 (-12.26)

**What's worse:**
- p1x0=0.96 (higher loop prob in state 1) — worse
- p1x1=0.74 (too low) — worse
- 4-transition state 0 — worse

**Key insight:** The scores are quite noisy (same machine scored -12.64 vs champion's -13.05). We need to keep resampling the champion region. The landscape around p1x1=0.76-0.79, p1x2=0.87-0.90 seems promising.

**Strategy:**
- Elitism: keep champion
- Exploitation: sample densely around p1x1=0.76-0.79, p1x2=0.87-0.90
- Try p1x2=0.86, 0.88, 0.90 combinations
- Try p0x2 threshold 5 vs 6 with champion's other params
- Small variations in p0x0 (0.84, 0.86) and p1x0 (0.95)

**Exploration (2 machines):**
- Try completely different: GoToColor(blue=3) as the action state
- Try 3-state with GoAway → Explore → GoToWhite pipeline

# PROMPT SYSTEME

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 30 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.41 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
